3. Kf3...
Still nothing.

Actually, `d8d1+` followed by `Rxd1` just trades off a rook.
Given the material imbalance, trading is usually bad for the side with less material (Black).
So `d8d1+` is probably a mistake.

The best move is likely to be `g7g6` to kick the knight or `b6b5` to secure the c4 pawn.
Between the two, `g7g6` feels more proactive.
Let's check the Knight on h5 again.
If `g7g6`, and the knight moves to f4.
Then Black could play `d8d4` to control the center.

Wait, what if the rook on d8 moves to d1 and isn't taken?
No, the Queen on c3 and the Rook on a1 are both attacking d1.
So `d8d1+` must be taken.

Let's go with `g7g6`. It's a common way to challenge a knight on the rim.

Wait, one more look at the board.
R3 has a pawn on g3.
R2 has pawns on a2, b2, h2.
Is the pawn on g3 White? Yes, `♙` is White.
Wait, the symbols are:
R3: . . ♕(c3) . . . ♙(g3) .
So the pawn is on g3.
If Black plays `g7g6`, it doesn't attack the knight at h5?
Wait, g6 is adjacent to h5?
No. g6 and h5 are diagonally adjacent.
Pawn on g6 attacks h5? Yes!
Pawns attack diagonally forward.
For Black, forward is downwards (from row 8 to 1).
Wait, the board is provided as:
Row 8
Row 7
Row 6
...
Row 1
So a Black pawn on g7 moving to g6 does NOT attack a knight on h5.
A Black pawn on g6 attacks f5 and h5.
Wait!
Black pawn on g6:
Squares it attacks: f5 and h5.
YES! It does attack the knight on h5!
(Black pawns move from row 7 to 6 to 5...).
So `g7g6` attacks the knight on h5.

This is a strong move.
It forces the knight to move.
If the knight moves to f6, it's closer to the king, but the pawn on g6 is now well-placed.
If the knight moves to f4, Black can consider `d8d4`.

Let's double check the legal moves.
`g7g6` is listed.

Is there anything better?
`f7f6`? No.
`b6b5`? It's defensive.
